<a id="eq:retained-branches"></a>
It remains to prove the weak inequality before averaging. We do so on open neighborhoods, to avoid differentiating across the sets where the maximizing branch changes. At a point in one of the open regions in <a href="#eq:barrier-open-cover" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:barrier-open-cover">[eq:barrier-open-cover]</a>, retain the candidates whose value is within $e_0R^{-4}$ of the maximum there. By continuity and finiteness, there is a neighborhood on which every discarded candidate remains strictly below a fixed retained maximizer and every retained candidate $v$ satisfies $$\label{eq:retained-branches}
 \widetilde u-v<2e_0R^{-4}.$$ The maximum of the retained family is still $\widetilde u$ throughout this neighborhood. At the origin use the continuous offsets from $V$ to make the same selection.

<a id="eq:common-branch-inequality"></a>
Every retained branch satisfies the common inequality $$\label{eq:common-branch-inequality}
 \Delta v\ge-4\pi\nu+4\pi\mathbf 1_{\{d<R\}}\mu_j
       -4\pi\widetilde p+\mathbf 1_{\{d\ge R\}}f(v)$$ on this whole neighborhood. For $v_1$, its old inequality suffices on $d<r$, and on $d\ge R$ use $f(u)\ge f(v_1)$. The only new source is on $r\le d<R$. Bad data there are paid for by <a href="#eq:barrier-added-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:barrier-added-error">[eq:barrier-added-error]</a>. On good data $v_2$ is a candidate, so <a href="#eq:retained-branches" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:retained-branches">[eq:retained-branches]</a> gives $$H'_j\le u-(\lambda_1-\lambda_2-2e_0)R^{-4}.$$ Moreover $h_l<7B/8\le d^4u\le C_{\mathrm{inv}}<h_h$. If $4\pi\mu_j>f(u)$, the high implication of <a href="#eq:inverse-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:inverse-comparison">[eq:inverse-comparison]</a>, at height $h=d^4u$, would give $$H'_j\ge u-\xi d^{-4}\ge u-16\xi R^{-4},$$ contrary to <a href="#eq:barrier-parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:barrier-parameters">[eq:barrier-parameters]</a>. Hence $4\pi\mu_j\le f(u)$, which supplies the required new density.

For $v_2$ its exact identity $\Delta v_2=-4\pi\nu+4\pi\mu_j$ suffices on $d<R$. At a remaining point where it is retained, the lower splice or the included barrier gives $\widetilde u\ge b_R$ and $d\le2MR$. Consequently $$d^4v_2\ge\frac{7B}{8}-2e_0(2M)^4>\frac{5B}{8}>h_l,
 \qquad d^4v_2\le C_{\mathrm{cap}}<h_h.$$ If $4\pi\mu_j<f(v_2)$, the low implication of <a href="#eq:inverse-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:inverse-comparison">[eq:inverse-comparison]</a> would imply $$H'_j\le v_2+\xi d^{-4}\le v_2+\xi R^{-4}
                <v_2+\lambda_2R^{-4}=H'_j,$$ a contradiction. Thus $4\pi\mu_j\ge f(v_2)$ there. The branch $b_R$ occurs only outside $R$ and satisfies <a href="#eq:common-branch-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:common-branch-inequality">[eq:common-branch-inequality]</a> by its subsolution property.

These comparisons concern zero-order densities almost everywhere on the same open neighborhood, including when it crosses $d=R$. The common reaction is $F(y,t)=\mathbf 1_{\{d\ge R\}}f(t)$. It meets the weak maximum rule’s hypotheses; near zero the common $V$ singularity cancels and the reaction vanishes. The rule therefore proves <a href="#eq:common-branch-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:common-branch-inequality">[eq:common-branch-inequality]</a> for $\widetilde u$ locally. A partition of unity proves it globally.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
###### Forgetting one observation.

Define $$u_{j+1}=V+\mathbb E[\widetilde u-V\mid\mathcal F_{j+1}],
 \qquad
 p_{j+1}=\mathbb E[\widetilde p\mid\mathcal F_{j+1}].$$ We justify the conditional integration of the weak inequality. For a fixed system the positive minimum packet width gives deterministic bounds for $\mu_j$, $K*\mu_j$, and its first derivatives. The initial truncated potential has the same bounds. Thus the initial offsets have deterministic local Lipschitz bounds; subtracting constants, taking finite maxima, and gluing on the fixed open cover preserve such bounds. Where $b_R$ is used, its offset from $V$ is smooth and separated from the origin. The errors remain bounded densities supported in deterministic balls. Conditional integration preserves these bounds. All fields and flags are jointly measurable; the spatial supremum defining the initial flag can be tested on a countable dense set. Fubini’s theorem therefore permits conditional integration against each smooth compactly supported test. A countable dense family of nonnegative tests, followed by local approximation, supplies simultaneous almost-sure weak inequalities for all tests.

The conditional tower gives $\mu_{j+1}$ as the new inner density, and convexity gives $$\mathbb E[f(\widetilde u)\mid\mathcal F_{j+1}]
 \ge f\bigl(V+\mathbb E[\widetilde u-V\mid\mathcal F_{j+1}]\bigr).$$ This proves the first line of <a href="#eq:barrier-invariant" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:barrier-invariant">[eq:barrier-invariant]</a> at $R$. The deterministic lower and upper bounds, the equality beyond $MR$, and the error support also pass to the conditional average.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
###### Total error.

For $r\le |y|<2r$, every master packet contributing to $\mu_j(y)$ has center in a fixed annulus comparable to $r$ and width comparable to $r^{1+w}$. Conditional Jensen, the packet bound, and [eq:annular-count,eq:rough-deficit] [eq:annular-count](#eq:annular-count), [eq:rough-deficit](#eq:rough-deficit) imply $$\norm{\mu_j(y)}_{L^2(\mathbb P)}
 \le Cr^{-3-3w}\bigl(r^{-3}+\sqrt{D_0r}\bigr)
 \le Cr^{-6-3w},$$ since $r\le s$ and $D_0\le Cs^{-7}$. The bad band event has probability at most $Cr^{25}$. Cauchy–Schwarz in the data and integration over the gain annulus therefore give $$\mathbb E\int\mathbf 1_{\{\mathrm{bad}\}}\mathbf 1_{\{r\le d<R\}}\mu_j
 \le Cr^{25/2+3-6-3w}=Cr^{19/2-3w}.$$ The sum over the geometric sequence of scales is at most $Cs^{19/2-3w}$. Conditional averaging preserves expected total mass. Together with the initial error estimate this proves <a href="#eq:barrier-error-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:barrier-error-mass">[eq:barrier-error-mass]</a> and completes the induction. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:limit"></a>
### The limiting field and its charge

We now prove the charge and profile assertions of <a href="#thm:priced-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:priced-deficit">2</a>; <a href="#prop:tf-identification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tf-identification">13</a> will identify the energy coefficient. The barrier is available at every intermediate observation scale, before all observations are forgotten. This lets us select conditional fields which satisfy both the local Thomas–Fermi equation and the small-error barrier.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Selecting conditional data

<a id="eq:intermediate-scale"></a>
<a id="eq:local-tf-approximation"></a>
Take an arbitrary sequence in <a href="#eq:scaling-regime" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:scaling-regime">[eq:scaling-regime]</a>, with an arbitrary minimizing index $n$ in each system. By <a href="#eq:rough-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:rough-deficit">[eq:rough-deficit]</a>, after passing to a subsequence we may suppose $$q_s:=s^3(Z-n)\longrightarrow q_*.$$ Choose integers $l\to\infty$ sufficiently slowly and a scale $j<J_*$ such that $$\label{eq:intermediate-scale}
 \frac1{2l}\le a_*:=\frac{r_j}{s}\le\frac1l.$$ This is possible because $r_0/s\to0$ and the radii are dyadic. In addition to the fixed parameters used in the barrier construction, apply <a href="#prop:inverse-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:inverse-comparison">9</a> at this scale with $M=l^2$, a fixed upper height greater than $C_{\mathrm{cap}}+1$, and lower height and tolerance sufficiently small in terms of $l$. On its good event, $$\label{eq:local-tf-approximation}
 \left|\mu_j(y)-k\bigl(H'_j(y)_+\bigr)^{3/2}\right|
 \le l^{-8}|y|^{-6}
 \qquad(r_j\le|y|\le ls).$$ Indeed, let $u=|y|^4H'_j(y)$ and $v=(|y|^6\mu_j(y)/k)^{2/3}$. The cap on $u$ excludes $v$ at or above the upper height: applying the high implication at heights approaching that endpoint would contradict the cap. Above the lower endpoint, the two implications at heights approaching $v$ squeeze $u$ to within the chosen tolerance of $v$. At or below the lower endpoint they bound $u$ above by that endpoint plus the tolerance. Thus $|u_+-v|\le h_l+\xi$, with both $u_+$ and $v$ in $[0,h_h+1]$; this uses no lower bound on $u$. Uniform continuity of $t\mapsto t^{3/2}$ on that interval proves the assertion after choosing $h_l$ and $\xi$ sufficiently small. The spatial range is covered because $4Mr_j\ge2ls$.

For each fixed $l$ these are fixed parameters of <a href="#prop:inverse-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:inverse-comparison">9</a>; its thresholds hold eventually and its exceptional probability tends to zero. A slow diagonal choice therefore makes <a href="#eq:local-tf-approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:local-tf-approximation">[eq:local-tf-approximation]</a> hold outside a set of probability tending to zero while also ensuring <a href="#eq:intermediate-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:intermediate-scale">[eq:intermediate-scale]</a>.

<a id="eq:selected-data"></a>
We can select data on which, in addition, $$\label{eq:selected-data}
 \int p_j\le1,
 \qquad
 s^3\int_{|y|>2R_0s}\mu_j(y)\,dy\le\eta_s,
 \qquad\eta_s\longrightarrow0.$$ The first excluded probability tends to zero by <a href="#eq:barrier-error-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:barrier-error-mass">[eq:barrier-error-mass]</a>. For the second, a master packet centered in $|x|\le R_0s$ is contained in $|y|\le2R_0s$ for all sufficiently far systems. The conditional tower and <a href="#eq:exterior-cutoff" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:exterior-cutoff">[eq:exterior-cutoff]</a> show that the expectation of its left side tends to zero, uniformly in $j$. Markov’s inequality supplies a deterministic threshold $\eta_s\to0$ with excluded probability tending to zero. These two events and the good comparison event have positive joint probability. Choose a point in their intersection where all the conditional identities and barrier inequalities hold. Only this finite-system selection is needed; every selected density still has total mass exactly $n$.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Compactness and the exterior boundary condition

<a id="eq:spherical-mean"></a>
On the selected data, suppress the sequence index and put $$F_s(x)=s^4\bigl(V-K*\mu_j\bigr)(sx),
 \qquad \rho_s(x)=s^6\mu_j(sx).$$ Then $$\Delta F_s=-4\pi Zs^3\delta_0+4\pi\rho_s,
 \qquad s^4H'_j(sx)=F_s(x)-1.$$ The total rescaled electron mass need not be bounded. We instead control the screened field through its spherical mean. Newton’s averaging identity and $\int\mu_j=n$ give $$\label{eq:spherical-mean}
 \overline F_s(d):=\frac1{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb S^2}F_s(d\omega)\,d\omega
 =\frac{q_s}{d}
   +\int_{|x|>d}\left(\frac1d-\frac1{|x|}\right)\rho_s(x)\,dx.$$ In particular $\overline F_s(d)\ge q_s/d$.

On every compact annulus, <a href="#prop:inverse-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:inverse-comparison">9</a> gives the uniform pointwise upper bound $F_s(x)\le1+C_{\mathrm{cap}}|x|^{-4}$ for sufficiently far systems. Together with <a href="#eq:spherical-mean" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:spherical-mean">[eq:spherical-mean]</a> and boundedness of $q_s$, this bounds $F_s$ in local $L^1$ away from zero: integrate $|F_s|=2(F_s)_+-F_s$ first over spheres and then over radii. Equation <a href="#eq:local-tf-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-tf-approximation">[eq:local-tf-approximation]</a> also bounds $\rho_s$ uniformly on these annuli.

<a id="eq:limit-pde"></a>
For completeness, choose a ball whose closure avoids the origin, and add to $F_s$ the Coulomb potential of $\rho_s$ restricted to a slightly larger ball. The sum is harmonic on the larger ball. The added potential and its gradient are uniformly bounded by the integrals of $|x-y|^{-1}$ and $|x-y|^{-2}$ against a bounded density. Interior harmonic estimates, using the local $L^1$ bound, give uniform bounds and equicontinuity on the smaller ball. A diagonal Arzelà–Ascoli argument now gives a subsequence converging locally uniformly on $\mathbb R^3\setminus\{0\}$ to a field $F_\infty$. The scaled error in <a href="#eq:local-tf-approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:local-tf-approximation">[eq:local-tf-approximation]</a> tends to zero locally uniformly, so the densities converge there to $k((F_\infty-1)_+)^{3/2}$. Hence $$\label{eq:limit-pde}
 \Delta F_\infty=4\pi k\bigl((F_\infty-1)_+\bigr)^{3/2},
 \qquad
 F_\infty(x)\le1+C_{\mathrm{cap}}|x|^{-4}.$$ The equation holds classically off zero. For example, bounded local sources first give local Hölder regularity of the field; the reaction is then locally Hölder, and interior Poisson regularity gives the asserted conclusion.

<a id="eq:limit-exterior-mean"></a>
The selected tail bound says precisely $$\int_{|x|>2R_0}\rho_s(x)\,dx\longrightarrow0.$$ Local uniform density convergence therefore makes $F_\infty$ harmonic on $|x|>2R_0$. For $d>2R_0$, the last term of <a href="#eq:spherical-mean" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:spherical-mean">[eq:spherical-mean]</a> is nonnegative and at most $d^{-1}$ times this tail mass. Consequently $$\label{eq:limit-exterior-mean}
 \overline F_\infty(d)=q_*/d\qquad(d>2R_0).$$

<a id="eq:limit-decay"></a>
These facts imply the uniform boundary condition $$\label{eq:limit-decay}
 F_\infty(x)\longrightarrow0\qquad(|x|\to\infty).$$ Here no positivity has yet been assumed. On large annuli rescaled to a fixed annulus, the upper cap bounds the positive part, and <a href="#eq:limit-exterior-mean" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:limit-exterior-mean">[eq:limit-exterior-mean]</a> bounds the integral of the negative part. Interior harmonic estimates thus give a uniform absolute bound for $F_\infty$ throughout the exterior. Given any sequence $R_i\to\infty$, its dilates $F_\infty(R_i x)$ have a subsequence converging locally on punctured space to a bounded harmonic function, with the same absolute bound on every punctured compact set. Its singularity at zero is removable, and Liouville’s theorem makes it constant. Its spherical mean is zero by <a href="#eq:limit-exterior-mean" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:limit-exterior-mean">[eq:limit-exterior-mean]</a>, so the constant is zero. If <a href="#eq:limit-decay" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:limit-decay">[eq:limit-decay]</a> failed, dilating at a sequence of offending radii and taking a convergent subsequence of the corresponding unit directions would contradict this conclusion. This proves uniform decay.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Transferring the positive barrier

<a id="eq:limit-positive-barrier"></a>
We claim that $$\label{eq:limit-positive-barrier}
 F_\infty(x)\ge B|x|^{-4}\qquad(x\ne0).$$ The field in this assertion is the field without the subtracted price. This distinction permits the reaction comparison below.

<a id="eq:barrier-correction"></a>
<a id="eq:barrier-transfer-inequality"></a>
Fix an outer radius $S>0$ and write $U_s(x)=s^4u_j(sx)$. Inside $a_*$, the distributional inequality for $U_s$ and the equation for $F_s$ contain the same point-nucleus and density $\rho_s$ terms; the inequality also contains the rescaled error from $p_j$. Outside $a_*$, <a href="#eq:local-tf-approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:local-tf-approximation">[eq:local-tf-approximation]</a> gives, once $l>S$, $$\rho_s(x)\le k\bigl((F_s(x)-1)_+\bigr)^{3/2}
                         +l^{-8}|x|^{-6}.$$ Define the nonnegative correction potential $$\label{eq:barrier-correction}
 L_s=K*\left(s^6p_j(s\,\cdot)
       +l^{-8}|\cdot|^{-6}\mathbf 1_{\{a_*\le|\cdot|\le S\}}\right).$$ Scaling <a href="#eq:barrier-invariant" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:barrier-invariant">[eq:barrier-invariant]</a>, subtracting the equation for $F_s$, and using $\Delta K=-4\pi\delta_0$ gives on $B(0,S)$ $$\label{eq:barrier-transfer-inequality}
 \Delta(U_s-F_s-L_s)
 \ge\mathbf 1_{\{|x|\ge a_*\}}\bigl[f(U_s)-f(F_s-1)\bigr].$$ In particular, subtracting $L_s$ cancels both the accumulated source error and the approximation error with the required sign.

Choose any $$c>\left(C_{\mathrm{inv}}S^{-4}
                   -\min_{|x|=S}F_\infty(x)\right)_+.$$ For sufficiently far systems, local uniform convergence on this sphere, the upper bound for $U_s$, and $L_s\ge0$ give $U_s-F_s-L_s\le c$ on the boundary. The same inequality holds throughout the ball. Indeed, test <a href="#eq:barrier-transfer-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:barrier-transfer-inequality">[eq:barrier-transfer-inequality]</a> with $(U_s-F_s-L_s-c)_+$. On its positive set, $U_s>F_s+L_s+c>F_s-1$, so the reaction difference is nonnegative. The test yields $$\int_{B(0,S)}\left|\nabla(U_s-F_s-L_s-c)_+\right|^2\le0.$$ It is legitimate on the full ball: the nuclear singularities in $U_s-F_s$ cancel, the remaining offsets are continuous and locally $H^1$, and the correction is the potential of a bounded compactly supported density for each fixed system. After cancellation all right-hand sides are locally bounded, including near zero, because the reaction term vanishes on $|x|<a_*$. Approximation therefore allows the compactly supported nonnegative $H^1$ test.

For each fixed $x\ne0$, $L_s(x)\to0$. The first density in <a href="#eq:barrier-correction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:barrier-correction">[eq:barrier-correction]</a> has mass at most $s^3$ by <a href="#eq:selected-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:selected-data">[eq:selected-data]</a> and is supported in $|x|\le a_*$. The second has total mass at most $$C l^{-8}a_*^{-3}\le C l^{-5},$$ by <a href="#eq:intermediate-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:intermediate-scale">[eq:intermediate-scale]</a>; its density near a fixed nonzero evaluation point is $O_x(l^{-8})$. Splitting its potential integral into a small ball around that point and its complement proves the claim. Finally, for fixed $0<|x|<S$ and sufficiently far systems, $$U_s(x)\ge B|x|^{-4}\left(1-\frac{a_*}{8|x|}\right).$$ Pass to the limit in $U_s\le F_s+L_s+c$, then decrease $c$ to its permitted endpoint. Letting $S\to\infty$ and using <a href="#eq:limit-decay" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:limit-decay">[eq:limit-decay]</a> proves <a href="#eq:limit-positive-barrier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:limit-positive-barrier">[eq:limit-positive-barrier]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### The singular profile and uniqueness

The dilation argument below is a direct form of the Sommerfeld comparison principle. The homogeneous zero-price profiles are covered by (Solovej 2003, Lemma 4.4 and Remark 4.5) <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[Solovej2003](#ref-Solovej2003)], including nonspherical fields. Here dilation removes the unit price near the singularity; a separate comparison then proves uniqueness for the priced equation.

<a id="stmt-lem-singular-profile"></a>
<div id="lem:singular-profile" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:sommerfeld-coefficient"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Unique decaying singular profile). *There is at most one classical solution $F$ of $$\Delta F=4\pi k\bigl((F-1)_+\bigr)^{3/2}
       \quad\text{on }\mathbb R^3\setminus\{0\}$$ which tends uniformly to zero at infinity and satisfies $c|x|^{-4}\le F(x)\le C|x|^{-4}$ near zero for some $c,C>0$. Every such solution has the uniform singular asymptotic $$\label{eq:sommerfeld-coefficient}
 |x|^4F(x)\longrightarrow
 A_{\mathrm S}:=\left(\frac{12}{4\pi k}\right)^2
       \qquad(|x|\downarrow0).$$ If a solution exists, it is radial.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-singular-profile"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-singular-profile</summary>

*Proof.* Let $a$ and $b$ be the lower and upper limits of $|x|^4F(x)$ at zero; the hypotheses give $0<a\le b<\infty$. Choose points approaching the upper limit and put $t$ equal to their radii. The dilates $u_t(x)=t^4F(tx)$ satisfy $$\Delta u_t=4\pi k\bigl((u_t-t^4)_+\bigr)^{3/2}.$$ They are bounded above and below by positive multiples of $|x|^{-4}$ on compact annuli. The local potential and harmonic compactness argument used above supplies a subsequential classical limit $u$ with $\Delta u=4\pi k u^{3/2}$. The definition of $b$ gives $u(x)\le b|x|^{-4}$ for every $x\ne0$. After taking a subsequence of the unit directions, equality holds at a unit point. The difference $u-b|x|^{-4}$ has a local maximum there, so $$4\pi k b^{3/2}\le12b.$$ The analogous dilation at points approaching the lower limit touches $a|x|^{-4}$ from above at a unit point and gives $4\pi k a^{3/2}\ge12a$. Since both constants are positive, $$b\le\left(\frac{12}{4\pi k}\right)^2\le a.$$ This proves <a href="#eq:sommerfeld-coefficient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:sommerfeld-coefficient">[eq:sommerfeld-coefficient]</a>. The contact argument does not assume radiality.

Let $F_1,F_2$ be two such solutions. For $\alpha>1$ and $\delta>0$, the function $G=\alpha F_1+\delta$ is an upper comparison function: $$\Delta G=\alpha\,4\pi k\bigl((F_1-1)_+\bigr)^{3/2}
       \le4\pi k\bigl((G-1)_+\bigr)^{3/2}.$$ If $F_1\le1$ the inequality is immediate; otherwise use $G-1\ge\alpha(F_1-1)$ and $\alpha^{3/2}\ge\alpha$. The common asymptotic coefficient makes $G$ dominate $F_2$ on all sufficiently small spheres. Uniform decay and $\delta>0$ give domination on all sufficiently large spheres. On the intervening annulus, testing with $(F_2-G)_+$ and using the nondecreasing reaction proves $F_2\le G$. Thus this inequality holds everywhere. Let $\delta\downarrow0$ and $\alpha\downarrow1$, and interchange the two solutions to obtain equality. Rotations preserve the equation and both boundary conditions, so uniqueness also gives radiality. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-singular-profile-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-singular-profile</summary>

*The charge assertion of <a href="#thm:priced-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:priced-deficit">2</a>.* The field $F_\infty$ constructed above satisfies the hypotheses of <a href="#lem:singular-profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:singular-profile">11</a>, by [eq:limit-pde,eq:limit-decay,eq:limit-positive-barrier] [eq:limit-pde](#eq:limit-pde), [eq:limit-decay](#eq:limit-decay), [eq:limit-positive-barrier](#eq:limit-positive-barrier). In particular a solution exists, is unique, and is radial. Its exterior mean determines $q_*$ uniquely by <a href="#eq:limit-exterior-mean" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:limit-exterior-mean">[eq:limit-exterior-mean]</a>. Moreover, at any fixed $d>2R_0$, $$\frac{q_*}{d}=\overline F_\infty(d)\ge Bd^{-4}>0.$$ Denote this uniquely determined positive number by $q$. The reaction density vanishes beyond $2R_0$, and radiality together with <a href="#eq:limit-exterior-mean" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:limit-exterior-mean">[eq:limit-exterior-mean]</a> gives $F_\infty(x)=q/|x|$ there.

Every sequence of systems and every choice of minimizing indices has bounded scaled deficits by <a href="#eq:rough-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:rough-deficit">[eq:rough-deficit]</a>. From any subsequence on which these deficits converge, the preceding construction produces the same unique profile and hence the same limit $q$. Therefore all scaled deficits converge to $q$: $$s^3(Z-n)\longrightarrow q>0
 \qquad\text{along }\cref{eq:scaling-regime}.$$ The argument allowed an arbitrary minimizing index at every stage, as required. The remaining coefficient identity in <a href="#thm:priced-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:priced-deficit">2</a> is proved in <a href="#prop:tf-identification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tf-identification">13</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:fixed-sectors"></a>
### Fixed particle numbers and the Thomas–Fermi coefficient

We first recover fixed-sector energy differences from the priced deficit proved in <a href="#sec:limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:limit">5</a>. This step uses monotonicity of the sector energies, without requiring convexity or requiring every sector to minimize at some price. The priced minimum is concave in the price, and its minimizing sectors bound its secant slopes even when the minimizing sector changes. Integrating the deficit law therefore determines the energy at those sectors; monotonicity then brackets the desired particle number. We identify the coefficient by the corresponding Thomas–Fermi calculation.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### Integrating the price and bracketing the sector

<a id="stmt-prop-fixed-sector-limit"></a>
<div id="prop:fixed-sector-limit" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:fixed-sector-limit"></a>
**Proposition 12**. *Let $q$ be the deficit coefficient in <a href="#thm:priced-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:priced-deficit">2</a>. For every sequence of integers $Z>m\ge1$ such that $m\to\infty$ and $Z/m\to\infty$, $$\label{eq:fixed-sector-limit}
 \frac{E_Z(Z-m)-E_Z(Z)}{m^{7/3}}
       \longrightarrow\frac37q^{-4/3}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-fixed-sector-limit"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-fixed-sector-limit</summary>

<a id="eq:priced-scaled-deficit"></a>
*Proof.* Fix such a sequence. At each fixed $t>0$, choose any index $n_t$ minimizing $E_n+tm^{4/3}n$, and put $$d_{Z,m}(t)=\frac{Z-n_t}{m},\qquad
 G_{Z,m}(t)=m^{-7/3}
       \bigl[P(tm^{4/3})-tm^{4/3}Z-E_Z(Z)\bigr].$$ The choice $s=t^{-1/4}m^{-1/3}$ satisfies <a href="#eq:scaling-regime" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:scaling-regime">[eq:scaling-regime]</a>, since $Zs^3=t^{-3/4}Z/m\to\infty$. The deficit assertion of <a href="#thm:priced-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:priced-deficit">2</a> therefore gives $$\label{eq:priced-scaled-deficit}
 d_{Z,m}(t)\longrightarrow qt^{3/4}
 \qquad\text{for every fixed }t>0,$$ for every choice of the minimizing indices.

<a id="eq:quantum-secants"></a>
For $0<v<t$, the minimizers at the two endpoint prices give $$\label{eq:quantum-secants}
 -d_{Z,m}(t)\le
       \frac{G_{Z,m}(t)-G_{Z,m}(v)}{t-v}
       \le-d_{Z,m}(v).$$ Indeed, evaluating the objective at $n_v$ gives the upper inequality, and evaluating the objective at $n_t$ at price $v$ gives the lower one. These inequalities do not require differentiability at a price with more than one minimizing sector.

<a id="eq:quantum-price-increments"></a>
<a id="eq:quantum-price-energy"></a>
Take a fixed partition $v=t_0<t_1<\cdots<t_r=t$. Summing <a href="#eq:quantum-secants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:quantum-secants">[eq:quantum-secants]</a> bounds $G_{Z,m}(t)-G_{Z,m}(v)$ by the two endpoint Riemann sums for the negative deficits. First use <a href="#eq:priced-scaled-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:priced-scaled-deficit">[eq:priced-scaled-deficit]</a> at the finitely many partition points, and then refine the partition. Since $qt^{3/4}$ is continuous, this proves $$\label{eq:quantum-price-increments}
 G_{Z,m}(t)-G_{Z,m}(v)
       \longrightarrow-\frac47q\bigl(t^{7/4}-v^{7/4}\bigr).$$ The behavior at zero price fixes the integration constant. The neutral sector is a competitor, whereas $E_{n_v}\ge E\ge E_Z(Z)-C$ by <a href="#eq:neutral-offset" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:neutral-offset">[eq:neutral-offset]</a>; hence <a id="eq:quantum-zero-endpoint"></a>$$\label{eq:quantum-zero-endpoint}
 -Cm^{-7/3}-v\,d_{Z,m}(v)\le G_{Z,m}(v)\le0.$$ For every fixed $v>0$ this gives $\limsup|G_{Z,m}(v)|\le qv^{7/4}$. Combining it with <a href="#eq:quantum-price-increments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:quantum-price-increments">[eq:quantum-price-increments]</a> and then sending $v\downarrow0$ yields $$\label{eq:quantum-price-energy}
 G_{Z,m}(t)\longrightarrow-\frac47qt^{7/4},\qquad
 \frac{E_{n_t}-E_Z(Z)}{m^{7/3}}
       =G_{Z,m}(t)+t\,d_{Z,m}(t)
       \longrightarrow\frac37qt^{7/4}.$$ Thus no assertion about the convergence of the priced minimizers as $t\downarrow0$ was needed.

Choose fixed prices $$0<t_-<q^{-4/3}<t_+.$$ By <a href="#eq:priced-scaled-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:priced-scaled-deficit">[eq:priced-scaled-deficit]</a>, their deficits are eventually smaller and larger than $m$, respectively. Thus $n_{t_-}>Z-m>n_{t_+}$. Monotonicity of the sector energies gives $$E_{n_{t_-}}-E_Z(Z)
       \le E_Z(Z-m)-E_Z(Z)
       \le E_{n_{t_+}}-E_Z(Z).$$ Apply <a href="#eq:quantum-price-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:quantum-price-energy">[eq:quantum-price-energy]</a> and let both prices approach $q^{-4/3}$. The two bounds converge to $\frac37q(q^{-4/3})^{7/4}=\frac37q^{-4/3}$, proving <a href="#eq:fixed-sector-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:fixed-sector-limit">[eq:fixed-sector-limit]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### The same charge in Thomas–Fermi theory

The classical weak-ionization law was established by Bénilan and Brezis (Bénilan and Brezis 2004, sec. 6, Corollary 5) <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[BenilanBrezis2004](#ref-BenilanBrezis2004)], following the bounds and chemical-potential integration argument of Lieb and Simon (Lieb and Simon 1977, Theorems IV.11–IV.12) <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[LiebSimon1977](#ref-LiebSimon1977)]. We give the argument in the present normalization to identify its coefficient with the charge obtained from the conditional quantum limit.

We allow real nuclear charges and real electron masses in this subsection. For clarity, the admissible finite-mass class can be written as $$\mathcal X=\{\rho\ge0:\rho\in L^1(\mathbb R^3)\cap L^{5/3}(\mathbb R^3)\}.$$ All terms of $\mathcal T_Z$ are finite on this class. For the direct term, interpolation gives $\rho\in L^{6/5}$, and the Sobolev inequality gives the embedding $L^{6/5}\subset(\dot H^1)^*$ and the finite Coulomb energy. For the nuclear term, use $|x|^{-1}\in L^{5/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ near zero and the finite mass away from zero. Thus this is precisely the finite-mass class in the definition of $E_Z^{\mathrm{TF}}(M)$.

<a id="stmt-prop-tf-identification"></a>
<div id="prop:tf-identification" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:tf-unit-deficit"></a>
**Proposition 13**. *With the two-spin coefficient $T$ fixed above, $$\label{eq:tf-unit-deficit}
 \lim_{Z\to\infty}
       \bigl[E_Z^{\mathrm{TF}}(Z-1)-E_Z^{\mathrm{TF}}(Z)\bigr]
       =\frac37q^{-4/3}.$$ The limit holds through real charges $Z>1$. Consequently <a href="#eq:tf-constant" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-constant">[eq:tf-constant]</a> holds for every fixed real $m>0$, with $$a_{\mathrm{TF}}=\frac37q^{-4/3}>0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-tf-identification"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-tf-identification</summary>

*Proof.* We identify the charge in the unit-price Thomas–Fermi problem, then integrate in the price and use the exact density scaling.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
###### Monotonicity in the electron mass.

The energy $E_Z^{\mathrm{TF}}(M)$ is nonincreasing in $M$. To see this without an attainment assumption, truncate any admissible density of mass $M_1$ to a large ball. The kinetic, nuclear, and direct terms converge to the original ones. If $M_2\ge M_1$, place the missing mass in a nonnegative smooth packet of diameter proportional to $R$, centered at distance $R^2$ from that ball. For a packet of mass $a$ and this spatial scale, its kinetic and direct energies are $O(a^{5/3}R^{-2})$ and $O(a^2R^{-1})$. Its nuclear term and its interaction with the fixed truncated density tend to zero as $R\to\infty$. The mass $a$ stays bounded during this construction. Taking first $R\to\infty$, then the truncation radius to infinity, and then the original energy down to its infimum proves monotonicity. The same argument shows that imposing mass exactly $M$ or at most $M$ gives the same infimum.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
###### The zero-price minimizer is neutral.

<a id="eq:tf-neutral-field"></a>
Apply (OpenAI 2026, Lemmas 7.1 and 7.2) to the single positive point source $Z\delta_0$. Their unconstrained problem on $L^{5/3}\cap(\dot H^1)^*$ has a unique minimizer $\rho_0$, with finite mass at most $2Z$. Its field $\phi_0=V-K*\rho_0$ satisfies $$\label{eq:tf-neutral-field}
 \rho_0=k(\phi_0)_+^{3/2},\qquad
 \Delta\phi_0=-4\pi Z\delta_0+4\pi\rho_0,\qquad
 c\min\left(\frac Z{|x|},|x|^{-4}\right)
       \le\phi_0(x)\le C|x|^{-4}.$$ The cited lemmas supply the lower bound, finite mass, and decay at infinity. For completeness, the upper bound is independent of $Z$: on a ball $B(x,R)$ with $R=|x|/2$, compare $\phi_0$ with $$v(y)=\frac{C R^4}{(R^2-|y-x|^2)^4}.$$ For sufficiently large universal $C$, direct differentiation gives $\Delta v\le4\pi k v^{3/2}$. The function diverges at the boundary, and the positive-part comparison for $\Delta\phi_0=4\pi k\phi_0^{3/2}$ in this ball gives $\phi_0(x)\le CR^{-4}$, as asserted.

<a id="eq:tf-neutral-minimum"></a>
Uniqueness makes $\rho_0$ and $\phi_0$ radial. Newton’s spherical-mean identity and finite mass give $$Z-\int\rho_0=\lim_{d\to\infty}d\phi_0(d)=0,$$ where the last equality follows from <a href="#eq:tf-neutral-field" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-neutral-field">[eq:tf-neutral-field]</a>. Therefore $\int\rho_0=Z$ and $$\label{eq:tf-neutral-minimum}
 E_Z^{\mathrm{TF}}(Z)=\min_{\rho\in\mathcal X}\mathcal T_Z(\rho).$$ There is no discrepancy between the variational classes here: every finite-mass admissible density belongs to the cited Coulomb-dual class, and the cited minimizer has finite mass and finite energy terms, hence belongs to $\mathcal X$.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
###### The unit-price minimizer.

<a id="eq:tf-priced-euler"></a>
Minimize $\mathcal T_Z(\rho)+\int\rho$ over $\mathcal X$. Removing density from the region $V\le1$ cannot increase this functional: the kinetic term, the contribution $\int(1-V)\rho$ there, and all removed direct interactions are nonnegative. We may therefore restrict to the ball $B(0,Z)$. Since $V\in L^{5/2}(B(0,Z))$, coercivity and weak lower semicontinuity in $L^{5/3}$ give a minimizer, and strict convexity of the kinetic term gives uniqueness. Denote it by $\rho_Z$ and put $\phi_Z=V-K*\rho_Z$. Nonnegative variations, followed by two-sided multiplicative variations on the positive density, give $$\label{eq:tf-priced-euler}
 \rho_Z=k\bigl((\phi_Z-1)_+\bigr)^{3/2},\qquad
 \Delta\phi_Z=-4\pi Z\delta_0+4\pi\rho_Z.$$ This holds globally: outside $B(0,Z)$ one has $\phi_Z\le V\le1$. The minimizer and its field are radial by uniqueness.

<a id="eq:tf-price-comparison"></a>
The following comparison is the unit-price case of the classical chemical-potential comparison (Solovej 2003, Lemma 5.1) <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[Solovej2003](#ref-Solovej2003)]; we include the short maximum-principle proof. The two fields satisfy $$\label{eq:tf-price-comparison}
 \phi_0\le\phi_Z\le\phi_0+1.$$ For the first inequality, on a positive set of $\phi_0-\phi_Z-\eta$, with $\eta>0$, the difference of the two reaction terms in [eq:tf-neutral-field,eq:tf-priced-euler] [eq:tf-neutral-field](#eq:tf-neutral-field), [eq:tf-priced-euler](#eq:tf-priced-euler) is nonnegative. Testing with that positive part gives a nonpositive squared-gradient bound and hence a zero test. The test is compactly supported because both potentials tend to zero at infinity. For the second inequality, $\phi_0+1$ solves the same unit-price equation away from the common point source, and the same comparison applies. At the origin the point singularities cancel; the remaining potentials are continuous and locally $H^1$, with locally $L^{5/3}$ source densities. Thus the compact tests are justified by $H^1$ approximation. Let $\eta\downarrow0$ to obtain <a href="#eq:tf-price-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-price-comparison">[eq:tf-price-comparison]</a>. The continuity and decay used here hold for Coulomb potentials of finite $L^1\cap L^{5/3}$ densities, as also proved in (OpenAI 2026, Lemma 7.1) <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[Foundation](#ref-Foundation)].

<a id="eq:tf-priced-mean"></a>
<a id="eq:tf-uniform-exterior"></a>
Write $$Q_Z=Z-\int\rho_Z.$$ By <a href="#eq:tf-price-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-price-comparison">[eq:tf-price-comparison]</a>, $\phi_Z\ge0$ and $\rho_Z\le k\phi_0^{3/2}\le C|x|^{-6}$. Newton’s identity reads $$\label{eq:tf-priced-mean}
 \phi_Z(d)=\frac{Q_Z}{d}
       +\int_{|y|>d}\left(\frac1d-\frac1{|y|}\right)\rho_Z(y)\,dy.$$ Finite mass gives $Q_Z=\lim_{d\to\infty}d\phi_Z(d)\ge0$. At $d=1$, the nonnegative second term and <a href="#eq:tf-price-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-price-comparison">[eq:tf-price-comparison]</a> give $Q_Z\le\phi_Z(1)\le C+1$. Using the exterior integral of $C|y|^{-6}$ in <a href="#eq:tf-priced-mean" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-priced-mean">[eq:tf-priced-mean]</a>, we conclude $$\label{eq:tf-uniform-exterior}
 0\le\phi_Z(d)\le\frac C d+Cd^{-4}.$$ Choose a fixed sufficiently large $R$. Then $\phi_Z<1$ on $d>R$ for every $Z$, so <a href="#eq:tf-priced-euler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-priced-euler">[eq:tf-priced-euler]</a> implies $\rho_Z=0$ there and $\phi_Z(d)=Q_Z/d$.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
###### Identification of the limiting charge.

Let $Z\to\infty$ through real values. On every compact annulus, [eq:tf-neutral-field,eq:tf-price-comparison] [eq:tf-neutral-field](#eq:tf-neutral-field), [eq:tf-price-comparison](#eq:tf-price-comparison) bound $\phi_Z$ above and below, and <a href="#eq:tf-priced-euler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-priced-euler">[eq:tf-priced-euler]</a> bounds its source. Local Poisson estimates give a locally uniformly convergent subsequence. Its limit $\phi_\infty$ satisfies <a href="#eq:limit-pde" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:limit-pde">[eq:limit-pde]</a> and $$c|x|^{-4}\le\phi_\infty(x)\le1+C|x|^{-4}.$$ The uniform estimate <a href="#eq:tf-uniform-exterior" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-uniform-exterior">[eq:tf-uniform-exterior]</a> gives decay at infinity. By the uniqueness in <a href="#lem:singular-profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:singular-profile">11</a>, this is the same field constructed in <a href="#sec:limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:limit">5</a>. Its exterior charge is $q$. At any fixed radius beyond both exterior supports, $Q_Z=d\phi_Z(d)\to d\phi_\infty(d)=q$. Uniqueness of the subsequential limit therefore proves <a id="eq:tf-unit-charge"></a>$$\label{eq:tf-unit-charge}
 Q_Z\longrightarrow q\qquad(Z\longrightarrow\infty).$$ The use of real charges is permitted by the arbitrary-positive-point-source hypotheses of (OpenAI 2026, Lemmas 7.1 and 7.2) <a id="pm-cite-52"></a>[[Foundation](#ref-Foundation)].

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
###### Other prices and their energies.

<a id="eq:tf-price-scaling"></a>
For $t>0$, let $\rho_{Z,t}$ minimize $\mathcal T_Z(\rho)+t\int\rho$, and write $Q_Z(t)=Z-\int\rho_{Z,t}$. Existence and uniqueness follow as above, now restricting to $B(0,Z/t)$. With $b=t^{-1/4}$, the transformation $$\widetilde\rho(x)=b^6\rho_{Z,t}(bx),\qquad
 \widetilde\phi(x)=b^4\bigl(V-K*\rho_{Z,t}\bigr)(bx)$$ gives the unit-price minimizer and field at nuclear charge $b^3Z$. Indeed, the transformed functional is exactly $$\mathcal T_{b^3Z}(\widetilde\rho)+\int\widetilde\rho
       =b^7\left(\mathcal T_Z(\rho_{Z,t})
                                      +t\int\rho_{Z,t}\right),$$ and this transformation is a bijection of admissible densities. Its mass is multiplied by $b^3$, so $$\label{eq:tf-price-scaling}
 Q_Z(t)=t^{3/4}Q_{t^{-3/4}Z}
       \longrightarrow qt^{3/4}
       \qquad(t>0\text{ fixed}).$$

<a id="eq:tf-priced-energy"></a>
Define $$\Pi_Z(t)=\min_{\rho\in\mathcal X}
                  \left(\mathcal T_Z(\rho)+t\int\rho\right),\qquad
 G_Z^{\mathrm{TF}}(t)=\Pi_Z(t)-tZ-E_Z^{\mathrm{TF}}(Z).$$ The endpoint-minimizer comparisons give, for $0<v<t$, $$-Q_Z(t)\le
      \frac{G_Z^{\mathrm{TF}}(t)-G_Z^{\mathrm{TF}}(v)}{t-v}
      \le-Q_Z(v).$$ The exact zero-price identity <a href="#eq:tf-neutral-minimum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-neutral-minimum">[eq:tf-neutral-minimum]</a> gives $$-vQ_Z(v)\le G_Z^{\mathrm{TF}}(v)\le0.$$ Consequently the same fixed-partition integration and then $v\downarrow0$ argument used in [eq:quantum-price-increments,eq:quantum-zero-endpoint] [eq:quantum-price-increments](#eq:quantum-price-increments), [eq:quantum-zero-endpoint](#eq:quantum-zero-endpoint) gives $$\label{eq:tf-priced-energy}
 G_Z^{\mathrm{TF}}(t)\longrightarrow-\frac47qt^{7/4},\qquad
 \mathcal T_Z(\rho_{Z,t})-E_Z^{\mathrm{TF}}(Z)
       \longrightarrow\frac37qt^{7/4}.$$ Each priced minimizer is also a minimizer at its own mass: an improvement at that mass would improve the priced objective. For fixed $t_-<q^{-4/3}<t_+$, <a href="#eq:tf-price-scaling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-price-scaling">[eq:tf-price-scaling]</a> places these two masses on opposite sides of $Z-1$ for all large $Z$. The mass monotonicity proved above and <a href="#eq:tf-priced-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-priced-energy">[eq:tf-priced-energy]</a>, followed by $t_-,t_+\to q^{-4/3}$, give <a href="#eq:tf-unit-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-unit-deficit">[eq:tf-unit-deficit]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
###### Every fixed removed mass.

For $m>0$ the density scaling $$\rho(x)=m^2\sigma(m^{1/3}x)$$ multiplies the mass by $m$ and satisfies $\mathcal T_Z(\rho)=m^{7/3}\mathcal T_{Z/m}(\sigma)$. It is a bijection between the corresponding admissible classes. Hence <a id="eq:tf-mass-scaling"></a>$$\label{eq:tf-mass-scaling}
 E_Z^{\mathrm{TF}}(N)=m^{7/3}E_{Z/m}^{\mathrm{TF}}(N/m),\qquad
 I_m^{\mathrm{TF}}(Z)=m^{7/3}I_1^{\mathrm{TF}}(Z/m).$$ For fixed $m>0$, the real charge $Z/m$ tends to infinity with $Z$. Applying <a href="#eq:tf-unit-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-unit-deficit">[eq:tf-unit-deficit]</a> proves <a href="#eq:tf-constant" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:tf-constant">[eq:tf-constant]</a> and the claimed value of $a_{\mathrm{TF}}$. This also completes the coefficient assertion of <a href="#thm:priced-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:priced-deficit">2</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### The specified order of limits

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-tf-identification-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-tf-identification</summary>

*Completion of the proof of <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* The joint conclusion <a href="#eq:joint-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:joint-limit">[eq:joint-limit]</a> follows from [prop:fixed-sector-limit,prop:tf-identification] [prop:fixed-sector-limit](#prop:fixed-sector-limit), [prop:tf-identification](#prop:tf-identification). To deduce the iterated limits, suppose first that, along unbounded integers $m_j$, the normalized inner upper limit exceeds $a_{\mathrm{TF}}$ by a fixed $\eta>0$. By the definition of a limsup, for each $j$ one may choose an arbitrarily large integer $Z_j>m_j$, in particular $Z_j/m_j\ge j$, with $$\frac{I_{m_j}(Z_j)}{m_j^{7/3}}>a_{\mathrm{TF}}+\eta/2.$$ This contradicts <a href="#eq:joint-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:joint-limit">[eq:joint-limit]</a>. The same selection applies if these inner upper limits are infinite. Likewise, a normalized inner lower limit less than $a_{\mathrm{TF}}-\eta$ along unbounded $m_j$ allows choosing $Z_j/m_j\ge j$ with normalized energies below $a_{\mathrm{TF}}-\eta/2$, again a contradiction. Therefore $$\limsup_{m\to\infty}
       \frac{\limsup_{Z\to\infty}I_m(Z)}{m^{7/3}}\le a_{\mathrm{TF}},
 \qquad
 \liminf_{m\to\infty}
       \frac{\liminf_{Z\to\infty}I_m(Z)}{m^{7/3}}\ge a_{\mathrm{TF}}.$$ Since the inner lower limit never exceeds the inner upper limit, both outer limits exist and equal $a_{\mathrm{TF}}$. The construction always chooses $Z$ only after $m$ is fixed; no convergence in $Z$ at a fixed $m$ has been assumed. ◻

</details>

    {
      "caption": "The overlapping definitions in one continuation step from $r$ to $R = 2r$. The shifted branches are $v_{1} = u_{j} − λ_{1}R^{−4}$ and $v_{2} = H′_{j} − λ_{2}R^{−4}$; the second branch is omitted on bad data. The source term changes on the shaded annulus $r ≤ d",
      "figure_id": "fig:barrier-step",
      "pdf_page": 22,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:barrier-step",
          "exact_quote": "The overlapping definitions in one continuation step from $r$ to\n    $R=2r$. The shifted branches are\n    $v_1=u_j-\\lambda_1R^{-4}$ and\n    $v_2=H'_j-\\lambda_2R^{-4}$; the second branch is omitted on bad data.\n    The source term changes on",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n    x=1cm,y=1cm,\n    font=\\fontsize{9}{11}\\selectfont,\n    line cap=round,\n    line join=round\n  ]\n    \\fill[orange!12] (1.6,0.65) rectangle (3.4,-4.65);\n    \\draw[orange!75!black,thick]\n      (1.6,0.78) -- (1.6,0.96) -- (3.4,0.96) -- (3.4,0.78);\n    \\node[above,text=orange!60!black] at (2.5,0.99)\n      {$r\\le d<R$: source gain};\n\n    \\foreach \\position in {1.6,3.4,4.9,6.4,9.3,11.8} {\n      \\draw[gray!50,densely dotted] (\\position,-0.12) -- (\\position,-4.65);\n      \\draw (\\position,-0.07) -- (\\position,0.07);\n    }\n    \\draw[-{Latex[length=2mm]}] (0,0) -- (12.9,0);\n    \\draw (0,-0.07) -- (0,0.07);\n    \\node[above] at (0,0.1) {$0$};\n    \\node[above] at (1.6,0.1) {$r$};\n    \\node[above] at (3.4,0.1) {$R=2r$};\n    \\node[above] at (4.9,0.1) {$1.04R$};\n    \\node[above] at (6.4,0.1) {$1.08R$};\n    \\node[above] at (9.3,0.1) {$MR$};\n    \\node[above] at (11.8,0.1) {$2MR$};\n    \\node[anchor=north east] at (12.9,-0.15) {$d=|y|$};\n\n    \\filldraw[fill=blue!7,draw=blue!65!black,thick]\n      (0,-0.9) rectangle (6.4,-1.8);\n    \\node[align=center] at (3.2,-1.35)\n      {$\\max(v_1,v_2)$\\\\[2pt]$d<1.08R$};\n\n    \\filldraw[fill=blue!7,draw=blue!65!black,thick]\n      (4.9,-2.2) rectangle (11.8,-3.1);\n    \\node[align=center] at (8.35,-2.65)\n      {$\\max(v_1,v_2,b_R)$\\\\[2pt]$1.04R<d<2MR$};\n\n    \\fill[green!7] (9.3,-3.5) rectangle (12.9,-4.4);\n    \\draw[green!35!black,thick]\n      (12.65,-3.5) -- (9.3,-3.5) -- (9.3,-4.4) -- (12.65,-4.4);\n    \\draw[green!35!black,thick,-{Latex[length=2mm]}]\n      (12.4,-3.95) -- (12.9,-3.95);\n    \\node[align=center] at (10.9,-3.95)\n      {$b_R$\\\\[2pt]$d>MR$};\n\n    \\node[anchor=north,align=center,text=black!70] at (6.45,-4.85)\n      {Schematic radial regions, not to scale.\\\\\n       The fields need not be radial.};\n  \\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A horizontal axis at the top represents $d = |y|$ directed to the right with an arrowhead at the far right. Ticks on the axis are labeled: $0$, $r$, $R = 2r$, $1.04R$, $1.08R$, $MR$, $2MR$. The interval between $r$ and $R = 2r$ is shaded, labeled above by \"$r \\le d < R$: source gain\". Below the axis are three horizontal rectangular shaded regions indicating definitions: (1) A top box spanning from $0$ to $1.08R$ labeled \"$\\max(v_1, v_2)$\" and \"$d < 1.08R$\"; (2) A middle box spanning from $1.04R$ to $2MR$ labeled \"$\\max(v_1, v_2, b_R)$\" and \"$1.04R < d < 2MR$\"; (3) A bottom box starting at $MR$ and extending to the right with an arrow past $2MR$, labeled \"$b_R$\" and \"$d > MR$\". Below the boxes, centered text reads: \"Schematic radial regions, not to scale. The fields need not be radial.\""
    }
